one key for London, Stockholm and Nijmegen · the Balbases 1677 table

London and Nijmegen → Copenhagen · syllabic nomenclator · 1676–78

Bernardo de Salinas and Pedro Ronquillo to Baltasar de Fuenmayor, 1676–78

Go to the full text ↓

Archives générales du Royaume, Brussels, Secrétairerie d’État et de Guerre, inv. nr. 2559 · DECODE R966–R984 and R1001, twenty letters · sixteen with a contemporary decipherment in the margin, four without

The Balbases key applies unchanged to all twenty: 88% of 23,463 groups get a value, including the two letters that DECODE lists with no decipherment; eighteen carry a contemporary decipherment in the margin, and with it all twenty now build sentences and are translated, with a few holes

Daniel Bourdeau · posted 21 September 2026 · updated 26 October 2026

Method: read from existing decipherment · Extent: partial (100% of the cipher) · On the map

Credits · Contemporary key or decipherment (key or decipherment used)

deciphered copy
On this pageThe lettersFour letters at first taken as unmarginedMethodWhat remains uncertainFull textsSources

At a glanceExisting decipherment · partial (100% of the cipher)

The letters
Twenty ciphered letters from Spanish envoys in Fuenmayor’s papers: eight from Bernardo de Salinas in London (August 1676 – March 1677) and twelve from Pedro Ronquillo at the Nijmegen congress (December 1676 – March 1678).
Where
Archives générales du Royaume, Brussels, SEG 2559; DECODE records R966–R984 and R1001. DECODE lists two of them as “non-decrypted”.
The cipher
The Spanish nomenclator of Balbases, shared by the northern envoys; Carpio in Rome used a different one.
How it was read
The Balbases key, rebuilt from his margins, was applied unchanged and gives values for 88.4% of the 23,463 groups. Sixteen letters have a marginal decipherment, which the key output confirms; six transcriptions had no group boundaries and were divided against the key’s vocabulary.
What it says
The four letters with no margin (R970, R973, R982, R984) are decrypted in gist only: the congress, English parliament and Charles II, subsidies, and the fall of Ghent.
Still open
11.6% of groups have no value, nearly all transcription noise. The four unmargined letters stand at R970 77%, R973 86%, R982 71%, R984 86%; their transcriptions still need correcting from the images.

02 Four letters at first taken as unmargined

R982 and R984 have no margin and were deciphered on the images with the key. R970 and R973 do have a margin, which DECODE recorded only in a comment; their readings follow it. Quotations are from the readings of 22–23 October 2026.

Ronquillo, Nijmegen, 26 February 1678 (R984)

Ronquillo replies to Fuenmayor’s reports on the assembly of the northern allies. Everything depends on the resolutions of the English parliament, and on whether Charles II will really break with France. Ronquillo, who knows that court, doubts it: [?no] me atrevere a hacer todavia juicio fijo ni de la paz ni de la guerra mientras no tuvieremos apariencias mas ciertas y seguras de las deliveraciones de Su Magestad Britanica. The Danes complain about subsidy remittances and say they can do nothing. The Elector of Brandenburg is in Pomerania. At the end, the Duke of Hanover’s intrigue, "esta trama", runs through his kinship with Madame.

Ronquillo, Nijmegen, 19 March 1678 (R982)

This comes ten days after the fall of Ghent. DECODE’s transcription is poor (full of "?"); re-transcribed from the soft scan, the letter reads with holes: the congress, Flanders taken by surprise, the two months that are lacking, the Brunswick princes, Con que de todas maneras estamos muy mal, and Ronquillo’s own melancolicas reflexiones.

Salinas, London, 15 September 1676 (R970)

Ya V.S. se hallara noticioso del fatal suceso de Mastrique: the fall of Maastricht, “quiso la fortuna cerrarnos esta puerta”. The discourses at the English court turn towards a peace, and the "progresos" on Fuenmayor’s side weigh on them. The minister has taken the news calmly. There is mention of the Danish fleet.

Salinas, London, 2 March 1677 (R973)

The English have made a beginning, no siendo tan malos principios quando los dan en negocio que tanto nos importa como retirar las tropas de Francia, en que me prometo persistiran. The preparations of recent months show that everything is in earnest. Talk treats the peace as good as made.

03 Method

  1. The Balbases key was used, with the four values added for Hernán Núñez (167 Suecia, 161 resolución, os para, 6+ o). It was applied without changes to DECODE’s transcriptions of all twenty records.
  2. Six records (R971, R974, R978, R979, R983, R1001) are typed character by character with no group boundaries, and at first gave 0%. Each line was rejoined and divided into groups by dynamic programming over the key’s vocabulary: fewest groups, with one-character groups penalised. They then decrypted at 90–94%.
  3. R970 and R973 write one sign as "Infinity" (∞), which is c before a vowel. R970’s transcriber also writes 5 where the key has a and b for que. These conventions account for most of its groups without a value.
  4. The margins were compared with the key output. They agree.
  5. Re-reading, 22–23 October 2026: every margin transcribed from full-resolution crops of the DECODE images (DECODE’s margin transcriptions checked against them) and aligned word by word with the key decode; the decode cut into words page by page (spaced.py); R982 and R984, and the transcriptions of R966–R970, R973, R976 and R977, corrected on the images (txi/). About 60 code values from the margins entered the key (keyx.py; coverage 93.6% to 95.4%). The readings first written in modern spelling were restored to the writers’ own from the key decode (tomavan, assi, haver, Magestad), with no word changed.

04 What remains uncertain

  • R983, the first page: the margin is faded almost to nothing on the DECODE scan, and groups l, c, 1q, L2, pA, 11 and 300 are not in the key, so its first passage has many holes.
  • R982 and R984, without a margin: the opening groups of R982 and about a dozen phrase-level holes, and about a dozen code groups in R984 (138, 91, 113, 460, 600, 109 and others) that no margined letter glosses. R982’s scan is soft; with these holes R982 reads at 91.9% of its words, under 95%.
  • Single words in R966, R967, R968, R971/R972, R976, R977, R979, R981 and R1001, where the margin is faded or blotted and the figures alone do not settle the word.
  • Grades: A for R966, R968–R975, R978–R981 and R1001; B for R967, R976, R977, R982, R983 and R984.

Full texts

The transcription and the decryption of every document on this page, so that the reading can be used and checked. Short files are shown here; all are in the repository.

R966, Salinas, 21 Aug 1676

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 21 Aug 1676: Charles II tells Salinas that an invasion of France would harm the peace and that the Maastricht and Philippsburg sieges will end the campaign; the King is in a bad humour over German successes; on the passports he favours the Münster precedent against the princes' new claims (Lorraine); no treaty talk until the campaign ends.

Spelling (v6, 23 Oct 2026): the writer's, as the cipher gives it; the modernised spelling of 22 Oct was restored letter by letter from the key decode (read2/R966.txt) with research/reading_audit/v6_charalign.py: where the key's letters spell the same word another way they are taken (tomavan, assi, devia, aora, quando, haver), editorial accents are taken off, and ñ is kept where the key writes n (it does not distinguish them). Code words, supplied and bracketed words keep their form without accents. No word was changed. Punctuation added. Modern spelling is only in the English file. Words in ⟨ ⟩ are written in clear in the letter and lead into the cipher; [...] = hole; [?word] = uncertain. -->

# R966, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 21 Aug 1676

## Reading

⟨... de lo mucho que se puede hacer.⟩ Aunque en una larga conferencia que tuve con el Rey me dijo que [la] entrada
en Francia antes dañaria a la paz que adelantarla, con que, deviendolo [...] creer al reves, lo devamos suponer por
lo mejor; y diciendole yo que esotro sitio no se podia excusar, dijo que con estas dos plazas que se tomavan acabara
la canpaña, cuyos dictamenes malignos temo no influya al Sobrino.

El Rey esta estos dias de mal humor, y se cree tener parte la fortuna de alemanes; pues siendo assi que no ay otro
discurso que el de estos sitios, contando hasta el ultimo mosquetero que se pierde en ella, ha callado las noticias
favorables, lo que Courtin no ha hecho con mas galanteria.

En el negocio de la protesta no se ha buelto a hablar mas; y en el de los pasaportes hable yo al Rey, que me
participo los que se havian ya permutado, y que habia escrito a Francia sobre la pretension de essotros principes, y en
particular del de Lorena, haciendo los oficios de buen medianero; pero discurio conmigo el que se devia seguir el pie
del congreso de Munster, y que si querian introducir novedades, que eran en perjuicio de los reyes, con que ni el
Emperador ni el [Rey] nuestro lo devian apoyar. A que yo replique lo que me parecio favorable al de Lorena, que es por
quien avogamos, en que veremos lo que se resuelve; con que se nos ira la resta del verano, que no causara novedad a
este Rey, pues el mismo me dijo que no se podia entrar en ningun discurso de tratado asta ver los ultimos tercios de
la canpaña. ⟨No ofreciendose que añadir de aqui ...⟩

## Notes

- The margin on f. 1 is very faint (the ink has almost gone); lines 1-5, 8 and 9 are legible ("Aunque en una larga
  conferencia que tuve con el Rey me dijo que entra... / en G... antes dañaría / a la paz que adelantarla / ... suponer
  por lo mejor / y diciéndole yo que esotro sitio no se..."). The ff. 2-3 margins are clear and agree with the key decode
  throughout.
- "en Francia": DECODE read the group as `bes`; on the image it is `bef` with the long crossing f, the same form as
  `bef` = Francia on f. 2 ("a Francia sobre"). The faint margin word begins with a capital ("en F[rancia]"; DECODE
  "grande?"). Salinas reports Charles II's view that an invasion of France would harm the peace rather than advance it.
- "debiéndolo [...] creer": groups `58 qe B` before `g 66 81` (= c-re-er); `qe` is unread and the margin is illegible here.
- Code words fixed from the margin (all new to keyx.py): `ers` = jo (dijo, 3 places; DECODE split it as "es 8"/"er s"),
  `bel` = favor (2), `fes` = noticia (1), `fis` = negocio (1), `fan` = oficio (1), `fa` = novedad (2), `do` = Lorena (2),
  `121` = congreso (1), `dos` = Münster (1), `up` = Emperador (1), `fe` = nuestro (key nue[stro]; the margin expands it as "el Rey mi señor"; R969 has `fe ▽ B` = nuestras), `89` = alemanes
  (1; key null), `84` = ur (Courtin), `82` = ir (seguir), `ug` = hu (humor), `59` = lu (influya; key null), `g` = c (2:
  dictámenes, creer), `bi` = ne (dictámenes), `K` = j (2: mejor, perjuicio), `I` = i (2: perjuicio, irá), `140` =
  particular, `cal` = había.
- Struck groups `1+3+`, `1+6+`, `f+`, `m` are nulls (mosque[1+6+]tero, últi[m]o ...). `mod ? nit 1+3+` at the head of the
  passage are nulls; `97` = aunque (confirmed in R967, margin "aunque yo preveo").
- "últimos tercios": the figures give te-r-ci-os (with DECODE's `ec` corrected to `oc` = mo); the margin writes
  "términos". The figures are followed.
- "ningún discurso": the cipher has `fos` before "discurso"; the margin writes "discurso alguno". In R969 `fos` + a =
  "ninguna" (margin "a ninguna plática"), so `fos` = ningún (2 places); the figures are followed.
- "en ella" follows the margin (the figures give e-ll-l-s).
- "acabara la campaña" follows the figures (margin "acabaría"). "se podía" (figures po-a-i-a; margin "podría").
- The Sobrino is William of Orange, Charles II's nephew; Courtin is the French ambassador in London.
Translation

R966_en.md

<!-- English translation of read/R966_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R966, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 21 Aug 1676

⟨... of the much that can be done.⟩ Although, in a long conference I had with the King, he told me that [the] entry
into France would rather harm the peace than advance it, so that, though we must [...] believe the opposite, we
should suppose it for the best; and when I told him that the other siege could not be avoided, he said that with these
two fortresses being taken the campaign would end; whose malign counsels I fear may influence the Nephew.

The King is in a bad humour these days, and it is believed that the good fortune of the Germans has a part in it; for,
though there is no other talk than that of these sieges, counting down to the last musketeer lost in it, he has kept
quiet the favourable news, which Courtin has not done with more gallantry.

On the business of the protest there has been no further talk; and on that of the passports I spoke to the King, who
told me those that had already been exchanged, and that he had written to France about the claim of those other
princes, and in particular of the [Duke] of Lorraine, doing the offices of a good mediator; but he argued with me that
the footing of the congress of Münster ought to be followed, and that if they wished to introduce novelties, these were
to the prejudice of the kings, so that neither the Emperor nor our [King] should support it. To which I
replied what seemed to me favourable to the [Duke] of Lorraine, for whom we are pleading; on which we shall see what is
resolved; and so the rest of the summer will go by, which will cause no novelty to this King, for he himself told me
that no talk of any treaty could be entered into until the last stages of the campaign were seen. ⟨Nothing else
offering to add from here ...⟩

R967, Salinas, 8 Dec 1676

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 8 Dec 1676: France has the English court bewitched; Parliament is awaited and may be prorogued; Salinas's memorial against levies for France was answered harshly, then patched up and both memorials suppressed; he holds firm, as Molina did.

# R967, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 8 Dec 1676

## Reading

⟨... que aunque sea dificil en lo fatigada que queda aquella corona,⟩ nada es hoy imposible en la fortuna que tiene la
Francia, y ninguna mayor que en tener tan hechizada esta corte, que por extorsiones que hacen no se da por sentida;
⟨pues sobre las quejas de estos pueblos a las piraterias de los corsarios franceses ... publicar placartes de otro,⟩
a todo esto el Rey y la corte de nada se dolia.

Pues estan ya en los principios de la turvacion, con que esperan al Parlamento, adonde se veran cosas grandes; aunque
yo preveo que, si entra furioso y el Rey no ve luz de conseguir algo, le prorrogara antes que tengan lugar de
adelantarse. Con que es problematico si nos conviene fiar en este tribunal o negociar con el Rey; y en la duda
[...] en uno y otro, para ver si por algun lado tenemos fortuna.

⟨... y las honras que siempre le he debido,⟩ pero viendose mi papel en el Consejo, voltearon el animo del Rey y le
persuadieron a que atajase con una respuesta aspera el curso de que se diesen papeles en esta coyuntura, pues eran
motivos para inquietar los animos de los parlamentarios; y con [?esto] resolvieron darme la respuesta disparatada, de
cuyo caso tuve luz, y me fuy al Rey y sus ministros y los puse de buena manera, con que capitularon, dandome
satisfacciones de palabra, y de que se suprimiesen anvas memorias. A que he replicado diciendo que de la mia habia ya
dado parte a Su Magestad, con que se anda buscando otra forma, en cuyo interin suspendo el remitir [la] respuesta,
aunque lo hare de una suerte o de otra para que V.S. la vea, hacia si o hacia todos. Y porque conoci ya esta forma de
obrar, y que intentaron la misma con el señor conde de Molina, me ha parecido sostenerlo con un poco de brio; pues si
nos dejamos caer en todas partes, nos despreciaran estos mas que ningunos, porque son mas sovervios que todos. De lo
que resultare dare parte a V.S., como se la doy de que en medio de estos dimes y diretes veo algunas sendas favorables
para nuestros negocios, que procuro seguir por si hallamos alguna del alivio que oy conviene, de que yre dando [aviso]
a V.S. ⟨Y no ofreciendose cosa mas particular ...⟩

## Notes

- Three cipher passages: f. 2 (two short pieces, margin beside both), f. 3 (top, margin) and the long one from the foot
  of f. 3 over ff. 4-5. All margins are clear and agree with the key decode; DECODE's margin transcription is badly
  garbled ("bolsearon", "capitularan", "tresando", "Lorana") and was redone from the images.
- Code words confirmed (proposed for keyx.py): `97` = aunque (also R966 l. 1, where the margin begins "Aunque"),
  `bes` = espera (also R969), `122` = conviene (2), `100` = algún/-a/-as (3), `fis` = negocio (2 here, also R966),
  `119` = Consejo, `180` = satisfacci-(ones), `bi` = ne (satisfacciones; also R966 dictámenes), `168` = Su Majestad
  (margin "S.Mg^d"), `bil` = forma (2), `141` = para que, `112` = conde (key null), `143` = porque (also R969), `dol` =
  medio, `fos` = ningun- (fos ▽ = ninguna, fos B = ningunos; also R966, R969), `59` = lu (luz twice, lugar; also R966
  influya), `82` = ir (conseguir, seguir), `77` = im (imposible), `it` = chi (hechizada), `es` = ja (atajase, dejamos),
  `ru` = yu (coyuntura), `nu` = fu (furioso, fui), `sa` = b (obrar), `98` = da (dando).
- Groups `mod`, `cit`, `3`, struck `1+3+`, `1+6+`, `7+6+`, `f+` and a struck `13` are nulls.
- DECODE label fixes (txi/R967.txt, read/R967_corrections.tsv): `for` = `fos ▽` (ninguna), `1+2+` = 42 and `1+8+` = 48
  (viéndose; crossed 4s), `1+7+` = 47 (persuadieron), a ▽ missed in "parlamentarios".
- "[...] en uno y otro": the cipher group is `C_`, the margin word is unclear (looks like "navego"/"nadaso"); not read.
- "con [?esto] resolvieron": the margin writes "esto"; the figure looks like `bi` with a dot, which elsewhere is "ne".
- "el remitir [la] respuesta" and "iré dando [aviso]": the figures give remitir-di(?)-respuesta and do-n-do-a-V.S.; the
  margin has "el remitir otra respuesta" and "iré dando a V.S.".
- The Conde de Molina was the previous Spanish ambassador in London. The "memorias" are Salinas's memorial against
  English levies for France and the rival paper; the Parliament was due in February 1677.

# R967, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 8 Dec 1676

⟨... that although it may be difficult, so exhausted as that crown is left,⟩ nothing is impossible today for the
fortune France enjoys, and none greater than in having this court so bewitched that, whatever extortions they commit,
it does not take offence; ⟨for on the complaints of these people about the piracies of the French privateers ... to
publish placards of another's,⟩ at all this the King and the court resented nothing.

For they are already at the beginning of the turmoil, so they await the Parliament, where great things will be seen;
though I foresee that, if it comes in furious and the King sees no light of obtaining anything, he will prorogue it
before they have room to go further. So it is doubtful whether it suits us to trust this tribunal or to negotiate with
the King; and in the doubt [...] in both, to see whether on some side we have fortune.

⟨... and the honours I have always owed him,⟩ but when my paper was seen in the Council, they turned the King's mind
and persuaded him to cut short with a harsh answer the course of papers being given at this juncture, since they were
motives to unsettle the minds of the members of Parliament; and with [?this] they resolved to give me the absurd
answer, of which affair I had light, and I went to the King and his ministers and put them in a good way, so that they
came to terms, giving me satisfactions in words, and that both memorials be suppressed. To which I have replied that I
had already given an account of mine to His Majesty, so another form is being sought, in the meantime of which I
suspend sending [the] answer, though I will do it one way or another so that Your Lordship may see it, towards one or
towards all. And because I already knew this way of acting, and that they tried the same with the lord Count of
Molina, it has seemed best to me to hold firm with a little spirit; for if we let ourselves fall everywhere, these will
despise us more than anyone, because they are prouder than all. Of what results I will inform Your Lordship, as I do
now that amid these comings and goings I see some favourable paths for our business, which I try to follow in case we
find some of the relief that is needed today, of which I will keep Your Lordship [informed]. ⟨And nothing more
particular offering ...⟩

R968, Salinas, 6 Oct 1676

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 6 Oct 1676: The Hague fixes a day for the Nijmegen congress; William of Orange is expected in London this winter with powers to treat, and his marriage to York's daughter is talked of; Charles II's dealings would harm the Dutch and Spain alike.

# R968, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 6 Oct 1676

## Reading

⟨... en medio de tantas desazones como nos atrae la constitucion de los tiempos, y sobre todo la de nuestras cosas.⟩
Ya V.S. sabra la resolucion que se tomo en La Haya para señalar dia fijo para el congreso, y no dudara quanto se habra
[...] desde aqui, por lo que desean adelantar la paz antes que llegue la hora de juntarse el Parlamento, donde nuestro
partido podia tener mas fuerza; pero lo mucho que ay que vencer en la asamblea, y la necesidad de llamarla, esta me
promete que aun hemos de reconocer sus afectos.

Tambien habra V.S. entendido los juicios que se hacen en si vendra el Principe de Orange a esta corte; y lo que le
puedo asegurar es que este Rey ha dicho que le espera este invierno. Pero si a esta evidencia se juntase lo que añaden,
en que trae poderes para tratar, estara tan mal a holandeses como a nosotros, y nada vien lo que se discurre en el
casamiento de este principe con la hija del de York; y aunque todo esta en amago, ay grandes apariencias de verdad, y
en mi opinion es la mayor el modo con que este caballero se ha portado esta canpaña, con que coronara sus obras y
dejara menos dudosos a los incredulos. Gran parte tienen en las opiniones de estos los celos que les da la desgracia
de suecos, y el pensar que a mas largo termino quedaran fuera de Alemania, y el Imperio mas sobre si; con que cegaran
tambien a holandeses para arrastrarlos al precipicio. ⟨Que es cuanto se ofrece que participar hoy a V.S. ...⟩

## Notes

- Date: the letter is dated on f. 3 "Londres a 6 de octubre 1676" (and DECODE's own clear-text transcription says so);
  the batch list's "8 Dec 1676" for R968 is wrong.
- Margin: ff. 1-2 carry the decipherment beside the cipher; the upper half of the f. 1 margin is faded (lines 3-5
  partly lost); the f. 2 margin is clear and runs to the end of the passage ("al precipicio"); f. 3 has no margin.
- "cuánto se habrá [...] desde aquí": figures `ag 10 65 ue C+ 48` = ha-b-ra-[ue]-ll-do; the margin word before "mucho
  desde aquí" is faded. Not read.
- "sus afectos": the figure is `bi` (+ s); elsewhere `bi` = ne (R966 dictámenes, R967 satisfacciones). DECODE seems to
  merge two signs under `bi`; the margin "afectos" is followed.
- Code words confirmed by the margin (proposed for keyx.py): `cep` = junta (2: juntarse, se juntase), `fo` = necesidad,
  `bon` = fuerza (struck 43 after it), `dal` = mucho (also R969), `cil` = holande(ses) (2), `cen` = hija, `cef` =
  Imperio (key null), `97` = aunque (3rd letter), `bes` = espera, `fe` = nuestro, `es` = ja (dejará), `▽`/`@` = a.
- DECODE underscored labels (`1_5_3_`, `6_3_` ...) mark groups the decipherer underlined; their values are the plain
  ones (153 = rey, 63 = no ...). txi/R968.txt strips the underscores; no other label change.
- "este caballero" is the Prince of Orange (his 1676 campaign; marriage to Mary, daughter of the Duke of York, 1677).
  "suedeses" in the figures (= suecos).
Translation

<!-- English translation of read/R968_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R968, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 6 Oct 1676

⟨... amid so many troubles as the state of the times brings us, and above all that of our affairs.⟩ Your Lordship
will already know the resolution taken at The Hague to fix a day for the congress, and will not doubt how much it will
have been [...] from here, because they wish to advance the peace before the hour comes for Parliament to meet, where
our party could have more strength; but the great deal that has to be overcome in that assembly, and the need to call
it, this promises me that we shall yet come to know their good will.

Your Lordship will also have heard the opinions held on whether the Prince of Orange will come to this court; and
what I can assure you is that this King has said he expects him this winter. But if to this evidence were added what
they add, that he brings powers to treat, it will be as bad for the Dutch as for us, and the talk of this prince's
marriage to the Duke of York's daughter not at all good; and although everything is still a threat, there are great
appearances of truth, and in my opinion the greatest is the way this gentleman has behaved this campaign, with which
he will crown his works and leave the incredulous less in doubt. A large part in their opinions is played by the
jealousy the misfortune of the Swedes gives them, and the thought that in the longer term they will be left out of
Germany, and the Empire more its own master; with which they will also blind the Dutch so as to drag them over the
precipice. ⟨Which is all that offers to tell Your Lordship today ...⟩

R969, Salinas, 8 Sep 1676

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 8 Sep 1676: after the fall of Maastricht peace talk grows and Van Beuningen leans to it; Salinas's orders are too limited to negotiate; Lorraine's minister to be treated as ambassador (precedent of Serinchamps); the other princes' claims depend on the Emperor; France's answer awaited.

<!-- Reading of the ciphered passages, built 22 Oct 2026 by Claude Opus 5.5 (ronq batch): the key decode (read/R969.dec.txt, keyx.py) aligned word by word with the contemporary decipherment in the left margin, transcribed from the DECODE images (DECODE's margin transcription checked against them). Spelling (v6, 23 Oct 2026): the writer's, as the cipher gives it; the modernised spelling of 22 Oct was restored letter by letter from the key decode (read2/R969.txt) with research/reading_audit/v6_charalign.py: where the key's letters spell the same word another way they are taken (tomavan, assi, devia, aora, quando, haver), editorial accents are taken off, and ñ is kept where the key writes n (it does not distinguish them). # R969, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 8 Sep 1676

## Reading

⟨... con la inconsolable fatalidad del suceso de Maastricht, que V.S. tendra mas por menor que yo, en que no
discurrire, pues aun para esto falta el animo.⟩ Con este motivo se refuerzan los discursos de la paz, que son los que
continuamente me tiene este Rey en conferencias publicas y secretas, a que no ayuda poco la inclinacion de Van
Beuningen, que siempre ha pecado por este lado; pero nunca la veo mas necesaria que oy, segun mi sentir y el poco
conocimiento que tengo de la mala planta en que estan en todas partes nuestras cosas, y la peor de todas la de Madrid,
que V.S. experimenta como todos.

Cuya materia se pudiera esforzar mucho en esta corte, pero [no] ignora tanpoco cuan limitadas nos vienen las ordenes
para poderse arrojar a ninguna platica. No obstante los esfuerzos de este Rey para encarrilar el congreso, nacen cada
dia nuevos inconvenientes; y sobre el mismo que oy queda en pie de los limites, ay los que V.S. save de los
pasaportes, en que reconozco que en lo que toca al Duque de Lorena se conseguira el que su ministro sea tratado de
embajador, porque veo a este Rey inclinado a ello, y convencidos los ministros de los ejemplares que produjo
Serinchamps; pero en quanto a los demas principes no se arrostra, y ha de haber grandes inconvenientes si Su Majestad
Cesarea no toma la mano, pues su ejemplar decidira este punto, como a quien mira mas la prerogativa que solicitan
estos principes; sobre que se espera la respuesta de Francia. ⟨Ayer dio la señora Duquesa de York otra duquesita a
este reino ...⟩

## Notes

- The margin is clear on ff. 1-2 and runs over to the foot of f. 2 ("... sobre que se espera la respuesta de Francia");
  f. 3 carries only the last four cipher lines, with no margin. Margin and key decode agree throughout.
- The sign DECODE calls "Water" is a small triangle ▽ = a (5 places: secret-a-s, necesari-a, nuestr-a-s, que-a-n = cuán,
  tr-a-do; also fos-a = ninguna).
- Code words fixed from the margin: `175` = secret(a), `fi` = necesari(a), `fe` = nuestr(o) (key nue), `182` = siempre,
  `dus` = materia (key), `dal` = mucho, `fos` = ningun(o) (also R966), `bos` = esfuerzo, `121` = congreso (also R966),
  `123` = convenien(te) (2: inconvenientes twice), `con` = haber, `170` = S.M. Cesárea (margin "S.M.Ces."), `bes` =
  espera, `ep` = Duque (key duq), `do` = Lorena (also R966), `des` = ministro (key), `80` = ar (arrostra, arrojar),
  `S` = a (2: "a que", "a ello"), `ru` = yu (ayuda), `ox` = mo (como), `I` = i (ignora, conseguirá; also R966), `es` =
  jo/ja (produjo, arrojar; cf. `ers` = jo in R966), `es+` (DECODE "es r+") = jem (ejemplares, ejemplar).
- "pero [no] ignora": the figures give only `ed 68 I 16 63 65` = pe-ro-i-g-no-ra; "no" is from the margin.
- "nuevos": `64 m 43 B` = nu-[null]-vo-s. "Madrid": `ac a 67 e` = ma-[null]-i-d (the r is not written).
- Struck or flagged groups are nulls: `mod 288` at the head of the passage, `*` 660 280 xa yut bon inside "se
  refuerzan" (margin "se refuerzan"; the groups are not split), `770`, `51`, `2+8+`, `m`, and `1+3+ 5+5+ de x+_` closing
  the passage.
- "poco conocimiento": `od xo 1+6+ 63 xi ic 76 73` = po-co-[struck]-no-ci-mi-en-to; the struck group stands for the
  second "co".
- "en pie": the figures look like `ic 13` (= mi-e); the margin writes "en pie". "de embajador porque": the figures give
  `en 8 31 143` before "veo"; the margin writes "de Embaj.r porque", so `143` covers -dor porque or similar.
- "Serinchamps": the figures give se-ra-in-cha-n-s, the margin "Serainchan". Charles-Henri de Serinchamps was the Duke
  of Lorraine's envoy at Münster; his precedents are what convinced the English ministers.
- "encarrilar": figures en-ca-i-la-r; the margin's word is unclear ("encaminar"/"encarrilar").
Translation

R969_en.md

<!-- English translation of read/R969_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R969, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 8 Sep 1676

⟨... with the inconsolable fatality of the event at Maastricht, which Your Lordship will have in more detail than I,
and on which I will not dwell, for even for this the spirit is lacking.⟩ On this occasion the talk of peace grows
stronger, which is what this King continually holds with me in public and secret conferences, to which the
inclination of Van Beuningen, who has always erred on this side, contributes not a little; but I never saw it more
necessary than today, in my opinion and with the little knowledge I have of the bad footing on which our affairs stand
everywhere, and worst of all that of Madrid, which Your Lordship experiences like everyone.

This matter could be pressed hard at this court, but Your Lordship is [not] unaware either of how limited the orders
reach us for venturing on any negotiation. Notwithstanding this King's efforts to put the congress on its track, new
difficulties arise every day; and on top of the one that still stands today over the limits, there are those Your
Lordship knows of over the passports, in which I recognise that, as regards the Duke of Lorraine, it will be obtained
that his minister be treated as ambassador, because I see this King inclined to it, and the ministers convinced by the
precedents that Serinchamps produced; but as for the other princes, no one will face it, and there must be great
difficulties if His Imperial Majesty does not take the lead, for his example will decide this point, as one who looks
more to the prerogative these princes solicit; on which the answer from France is awaited. ⟨Yesterday the Duchess of
York gave this kingdom another little duchess ...⟩

R970, Salinas, 15 Sep 1676

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 15 Sep 1676: after the fall of Maastricht nothing can be expected of Orange and England drifts towards peace; Salinas watches Van Beuningen; the Danish envoy fears him and Bergeyck and has asked the King of Denmark to keep Salinas in London.

Transcription

<!-- Reading of the ciphered passages, built 22 Oct 2026 by Claude Opus 5.5 (ronq batch): the key decode (read/R970.dec.txt, keyx.py) aligned word by word with the contemporary decipherment in the left margin, transcribed from the DECODE images. DECODE's transcriber put this margin in #COMMENT blocks, not in the margin lines, so the letter had been listed as one without a decipherment. Spelling (v6, 23 Oct 2026): the writer's, as the cipher gives it; the modernised spelling of 22 Oct was restored letter by letter from the key decode (read2/R970.txt) with research/reading_audit/v6_charalign.py: where the key's letters spell the same word another way they are taken (tomavan, assi, devia, aora, quando, haver), editorial accents are taken off, and ñ is kept where the key writes n (it does not distinguish them). Code words, supplied and bracketed words keep their form without accents. No word was changed. Punctuation added. Modern spelling is only in the English file. Words in ⟨ ⟩ are written in clear in the letter and lead into the cipher; [...] = hole; [?word] = uncertain. -->

# R970, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 15 Sep 1676

⟨... avisare a V.S. para que sepa donde me han de hallar sus ordenes, y en el interim le suplico no me niegue aqui todas
las que fueren de su servicio.⟩ Ya V.S. se hallara noticioso del fatal suceso de Mastrique, porque no quede recurso a
nuestras esperanzas; quiso la fortuna cerrarnos esta puerta, dejandonos por alivio el desengaño de cuan poco se puede
esperar del Principe de Orange y de las armas que el mandare, con que este conocimiento, mas a la necesidad precisa,
nos llevara a gran paso a la paz, que es lo que estos quieren y a cuyo fin dirigen estos movimientos. Con que oy se
renuevan los discursos a ella, y a mi me tiran cada dia flechas, que es preciso reparar hasta que lleguen ordenes
claras que nos guien a los aciertos.

No mueven menos a estos animos los progresos de essa parte, pues, como V.S. conoce, ni los desean ni les esta vien. Yo
les aconsejo que aprieten la mano, porque la sopa no les puede durar, segun veo nuestras cosas y el animo de
holandeses, cuyo ministro en esta corte ha logrado con este suceso lo que ha deseado, asi por lo mal que quiere al
Principe como por lo mucho que anhela por la paz. Yo le traigo muy a la vista, y por no desconfiarle, a que me hurte el
cuerpo, le llevo el humor, y tengo la mano porque no caiga en alguna liviandad. El ministro de esse Rey le teme mucho, y
no menos a Bergeyck, porque le conocio aqui; con que le ha alarmado su venida, por ser cierto que los dos juntos haran
alguna ligereza. Con que me dice que ha escrito a Su Magestad danesa para que repare este golpe y haga oficios para que
yo quede aqui, que no he savido hasta que se lo dijo a Manuel de Fonseca. Y en este instante me acava de advertir que
anoche tuvo Van Beuningen una larga conferencia con el Rey, con que voy a reconocer lo que pasa.

## Notes

- Margin found: 5022, 5023 and 5024 carry a full contemporary decipherment in the left margin (the last line of 5023
  runs across the foot of the page). DECODE transcribed it in comment blocks, not as margin lines, so the letter had
  been listed as one without a decipherment. The other pages have no cipher.
- Margin and key decode agree throughout; every word is carried by the cipher groups, the code words by the margin.
  The label fixes and the code words are listed in R970_corrections.tsv and R970_reread_log.md.
- "Su Majestad danesa": a code group for Su Majestad followed by "danesa" spelled out; the margin writes "S.M.D.".
- "acaba de advertir": the cipher has "acaba", the margin "acababa".
- "más a la necesidad precisa" is in both margin and cipher; the sense is "this knowledge, added to pressing
  necessity".
- Maastricht: William of Orange raised the siege in late August 1676. "Esa parte" is the Danish side of the Scanian
  war. The Dutch minister in London is Van Beuningen, named at the end.
Translation

R970_en.md

<!-- English translation of read/R970_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R970, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 15 Sep 1676

⟨... I shall let Your Lordship know, so that you know where your orders are to find me, and meanwhile I beg you not
to deny me here any that may serve you.⟩ Your Lordship will already have heard of the fatal event at Maastricht. So
that no resource should be left to our hopes, fortune chose to shut this door on us, leaving us as our only relief the
disillusion of how little can be expected of the Prince of Orange and of the armies he may command. This knowledge,
added to pressing necessity, will carry us at a great pace to peace, which is what these people want and the end to
which they direct these moves. So today the talk of it is renewed, and every day they shoot arrows at me, which I must
parry until clear orders arrive to guide us to the right course.

The progress on your side moves these minds no less, for, as Your Lordship knows, they neither wish for it nor is it
in their interest. I advise them to press hard, because the soup cannot last them, as I see our affairs and the mood
of the Dutch, whose minister at this court has obtained by this event what he wished, both from his ill will to the
Prince and from his great longing for peace. I keep him well in view, and so as not to make him distrustful, so that
he does not slip away from me, I humour him, and I hold him back so that he does not fall into some rashness. The
minister of that King fears him greatly, and Bergeyck no less, because he knew him here; so his coming has alarmed
him, since it is certain that the two together will do something rash. So he tells me that he has written to His
Danish Majesty to remedy this blow and to make representations so that I stay here, which I did not know until he
told it to Manuel de Fonseca. And at this moment he has just told me that last night Van Beuningen had a long
conference with the King, so I am going to find out what is happening.

R971, Salinas, 26 Jan 1677 (= R972)

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 26 Jan 1677 (same letter as R972): Temple's mission to The Hague won Orange over to the peace; Salinas fears the Anglo-Dutch project sacrifices Burgundy; Charles II says the peace must be made and that Flanders keeps frontiers; recruiting for France stopped in England but goes on in Scotland; Amerongen and Van Beuningen differ over a truce; a 2-3 month suspension of arms may be proposed.

Transcription

Spelling (v6, 23 Oct 2026): the writer's, as the cipher gives it; the modernised spelling of 22 Oct was restored letter by letter from the key decode (read2/R971.txt) with research/reading_audit/v6_charalign.py: where the key's letters spell the same word another way they are taken (tomavan, assi, devia, aora, quando, haver), editorial accents are taken off, and ñ is kept where the key writes n (it does not distinguish them). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are written in clear in the letter and lead into the cipher; [...] = hole; [?word] = uncertain. -->

# R971, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 26 Jan 1677

⟨... y que toda mi obediencia a su servicio desea merecerlo.⟩ No extraño lo embarazoso que le sera a V.S. essa
residencia, cuando ha de tratar con tan diferentes humores e intereses, y particularmente con el Obispo de Munster, a
quien tengo por caviloso y, como dicen, algo fourbe; pero consuelese V.S. con que en todas partes hay trabajo, y vaya
dando la buelta al norte, y reconocera como anda [?nuestra] lira, lo que pasa en Nimega y lo que sucede aqui,
procediendo la desorden del piloto que gobierna el timon, en que dicen las voces del pueblo hay otro que solo lo puede
remediar; en que las cartas de Madrid desengañaran a V.S.

Don Pedro Ronquillo ha estado estos dias con cuidado, sobrandole motivos; y aunque mis avisos no le habran sacado de
el, no obstante caminara con mas luz, pues procediendo sus temores de la ida a La Haya del embajador Tenple, que dio
de recelar a el y a todos, he procurado descubrir todo el tereno que he podido, y he hallado que todo se encamino a
reducir al Principe de Orange para que diese la mano a la paz, y que lo ha conseguido; pues desde este ultimo correo
de Holanda esta este Rey mas gustoso y satisfecho de dicho Principe, y el embajador Van Beuningen no menos, con su
inclinacion tan descarada a este fin.

Pero aun no es este todo el mal, pues si se encaminaran esos oficios por la senda derecha de la honra, pudieramos
esperar un buen ajuste para todos; pero temo, y no sin grandes fundamentos, que entre este Rey y holandeses nos han
[?proyectado] la paz que nos quieren dar, y que ha sido menester vencer al de Orange y ajustar sus intereses; con cuya
negociacion, y la prisa que se dan estos en Nimega para llegar a la sustancia, pasando sobre tantos inconvenientes y
formalidades de disgusto para las partes, van de carrera a la conclusion, y se prometen aqui conseguirla antes de la
canpaña, por lo que le importa a este Rey y por lo que lo desean holandeses. En cuyo proyecto temo siempre que nos
quitan la Borgoña, porque, habiendose pasado grandes discursos conmigo sobre esta materia, en que me vieron firme y
asentar por primer fundamento de la paz la restitucion de esta provincia, se han recatado de mi; y por ultimo andan
grandes demandas y respuestas de aqui a Paris.

Sobre que hable dos dias ha al Rey, y me dijo que la paz se habia de hacer, que el ni estava en estado de entrar en
guerra ni la queria, que Flandes habia de quedar con fronteras, que no nos lisonjeasemos con el Parlamento, porque
este no le obligaria a este empeño. En que dire a V.S. que las disposiciones que tenemos para este tribunal no pueden
ser mejores, pero por la misma razon temo que ha de haber tanta confusion que ha de obligar a este Rey a disolverle o
prorrogarle, con que nos quedaremos como nos estamos. No obstante, no pierdo de vista todos los oficios que puedan
fomentar las esperanzas; y para el punto principal, y que nos mira tan de cerca, el de las levas y reclutas para
Francia, he proseguido mis quejas sin rendirme al temor que me quisieron poner con la respuesta disparatada que se me
dio, en que no obstante se logro a lo menos el que en el reino de Inglaterra cesasen las reclutas; pero prosiguiendo en
Escocia, escribi el papel de que va copia adjunta al secretario de Estado, cuya respuesta vera V.S., tambien con la
carta que Su Majestad Britanica mando escribir a los estados de Escocia.

Vi lo que V.S. me participa del discurso que le tuvo Monsieur de Amerongen, y es lastima que una respuesta tan discreta
y prudente como la de V.S. se emplease en aquel sujeto, porque no es de los mas clasicos de Holanda, aunque es de los de
la mala intencion. Su dictamen no confronta con el de Van Beuningen, que esta aqui, pues este no es de la opinion de la
tregua, porque es mas [?menagero], y le parece que el mantener los aliados les pudiera causar algun gasto, con que este
se encamina con toda precipitacion a la paz; y, como V.S. dice, cualquiera sera mejor que una tregua. Pero lo que yo
creo es que, si faltase aqui a la avertura de la canpaña tiempo para concluir la paz, pudiera este Rey, instado de este
ministro, proponer alguna suspension de armas de dos o tres meses, pero mas no. Lo que puedo asegurar a V.S. es que
antes de un mes ha de reventar la postema, y que de todo lo que fuere ocurriendo le dare cuenta mas puntualmente,
solicitando siempre [de] [V.S.] las ordenes de su servicio.

## Notes

- R971 and R972 are the same letter: the seven DECODE images of R971 (5027-5033) and R972 (5034-5040) are pixel-identical,
  and both records date it Londres 26 January 1677 (the batch list's "1 Jan 1677" for R971 is wrong). The two DECODE
  cipher transcriptions differ only in the transcriber's labelling. One reading serves both.
- The margin runs beside every cipher line on ff. 1-6 and is clear throughout; it was read from the images in full
  (DECODE's margin transcription is garbled: "pilono", "temetes", "sonda", "Handes", "lafrastia" ...).
- The key decode on this transcription is noisy (DECODE split the groups oddly: `1 7` = st, `q+` = de, `§` = embajador,
  `W at e r` = "Water" sign + tratar); where it is legible it agrees with the margin word for word, e.g. "lo haya de le
  embax r te n p le" = "a La Haya del embajador Temple", "le pare f se el man tene los al i gul s les pu diar a ca 3 3 sa
  r b 0 ga s to" = "le parece el mantener los aliados les pudiera causar algún gasto" (the margin's "monseur" is a
  misreading of "mantener"; the figures decide).
- Code words confirmed: `dos` = Münster (also R966), `cil` = holande(ses)/Holanda (5 here), `bil` = formalidades
  (forma-, cf. R967 `bil` = forma), `fe` = nuestr(a), `dis` = monsieur, `163` = razón here (margin "por la misma razón"; keyx.py
  has 163 = suecia; both DECODE transcriptions read 163), `co` = haber, `bo` = empeño, `120` = congreso.
- "[?nuestra] lira": figures `75 45 fe 57 65` = an-da-nue-li-ra, margin "nro lira"; the sense ("how our lyre is
  playing") is idiomatic and not certain.
- "nos han [?proyectado] la paz": figures ha-n-p-ro-[null]-ta-do; the margin word is "projectado"/"protestado"; the
  figures support a word in pro-…-tado.
- "[?menagero]": figures me-na-[?]-o, margin "menageo" (French ménager, thrifty).
- "fourbe" is French (a cheat). The Bishop of Münster is Christoph Bernhard von Galen; Amerongen the Dutch envoy in
  Copenhagen; Temple the English ambassador at The Hague.

<!-- English translation of read/R971_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R971, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 26 Jan 1677

⟨... and that all my obedience to your service wishes to deserve it.⟩ I do not wonder how awkward that residence must
be for Your Lordship, having to deal with such different humours and interests, and particularly with the Bishop of
Münster, whom I take for a quibbler and, as they say, something of a cheat; but let Your Lordship console himself that
there is trouble everywhere, and let him turn round to the north and he will see how [?our] lyre plays, what is
happening at Nijmegen and what is happening here, the disorder proceeding from the pilot who steers the helm, for
which, the voices of the people say, there is another who alone can remedy it; on which the letters from Madrid will
undeceive Your Lordship.

Don Pedro Ronquillo has been anxious these days, with motives to spare; and although my reports will not have freed
him of it, he will nevertheless proceed with more light, for since his fears came from the journey of Ambassador
Temple to The Hague, which gave him and everyone cause for suspicion, I have tried to discover all the ground I could,
and I have found that everything was aimed at bringing the Prince of Orange round to lend a hand to the peace, and that
it has succeeded; for since this last post from Holland this King is better pleased and satisfied with the said Prince,
and Ambassador Van Beuningen no less, with his inclination so barefaced to this end.

But even this is not the whole evil, for if those offices were directed along the straight path of honour, we could
hope for a good settlement for all; but I fear, and not without great grounds, that between this King and the Dutch
they have [?planned] for us the peace they mean to give us, and that it has been necessary to win over the [Prince] of
Orange and settle his interests; with which negotiation, and the haste these men are making at Nijmegen to come to the
substance, passing over so many difficulties and formalities displeasing to the parties, they are running to the
conclusion, and they promise themselves here to obtain it before the campaign, because of what it matters to this King
and because the Dutch desire it. In which project I always fear they take Burgundy from us, because, great discussions
having passed with me on this matter, in which they saw me firm and laying down as the first foundation of the peace
the restitution of this province, they have kept themselves guarded from me; and lately there are great demands and
answers going from here to Paris.

On which I spoke two days ago to the King, and he told me that the peace had to be made, that he was neither in a
state to enter a war nor wanted it, that Flanders had to remain with frontiers, that we should not flatter ourselves
with the Parliament, because it would not oblige him to this engagement. On which I will tell Your Lordship that the
dispositions we have towards this tribunal could not be better, but for the same reason I fear there must be so much
confusion that it will oblige this King to dissolve or prorogue it, so that we shall remain as we are. Nevertheless I
do not lose sight of any offices that may foster our hopes; and on the main point, which touches us so closely, that
of the levies and recruits for France, I have pursued my complaints without yielding to the fear they tried to put in
me with the absurd answer I was given, in which nevertheless it was achieved at least that the recruiting ceased in the
kingdom of England; but as it continued in Scotland, I wrote the paper of which a copy goes enclosed to the Secretary
of State, whose answer Your Lordship will see, together with the letter His Britannic Majesty ordered written to the
Estates of Scotland.

I saw what Your Lordship tells me of the discourse Monsieur d'Amerongen held with you, and it is a pity that so discreet
and prudent an answer as Your Lordship's should be spent on that person, for he is not one of the most classic men of
Holland, though he is one of those of ill intention. His opinion does not agree with that of Van Beuningen, who is
here, for the latter is not of the opinion for the truce, because he is more [?thrifty], and he thinks that maintaining
the allies might cost them some expense, so that he is heading with all haste towards the peace; and, as Your Lordship
says, any [peace] will be better than a truce. But what I believe is that, if time were lacking here at the opening of
the campaign to conclude the peace, this King, urged by this minister, might propose some suspension of arms for two
or three months, but no more. What I can assure Your Lordship is that within a month the abscess must burst, and that
of everything that occurs I will give you a more punctual account, always seeking [Your Lordship's] orders for your
service.

R972, Salinas, 26 Jan 1677

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 26 Jan 1677 (same letter as R971): Temple, Orange and the peace; fears for Burgundy; Charles II on the peace and the frontiers of Flanders; recruiting for France; Amerongen and Van Beuningen; a proposed suspension of arms.

Spelling (v6, 23 Oct 2026): the writer's, as the cipher gives it; the modernised spelling of 22 Oct was restored letter by letter from the key decode (read2/R972.txt) with research/reading_audit/v6_charalign.py: where the key's letters spell the same word another way they are taken (tomavan, assi, devia, aora, quando, haver), editorial accents are taken off, and ñ is kept where the key writes n (it does not distinguish them). # R972, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 26 Jan 1677

⟨... y que toda mi obediencia a su servicio desea merecerlo.⟩ No extraño lo embarazoso que le sera a V.S. essa
residencia, quando ha de tratar con tan diferentes humores e intereses, y particularmente con el Ovispo de Munster, a
quien tengo por caviloso y, como dicen, algo fourve; pero consuelese V.S. con que en todas partes hay trabajo, y vaya
dando la vuelta al norte, y reconocera como anda [?nuestra] lira, lo que pasa en Nimega y lo que sucede aqui,
procediendo la desorden del piloto que gobierna el timon, en que dicen las voces del pueblo ay otro que solo lo puede
remediar; en que las cartas de Madrid desengañaran a V.S.

Don Pedro Ronquillo ha estado estos dias con cuidado, sobrandole motivos; y aunque mis avisos no le habran sacado de
el, no obstante caminara con mas luz, pues procediendo sus temores de la ida a La Haya del embajador Tenple, que dio
de recelar a el y a todos, he procurado descubrir todo el tereno que he podido, y he hallado que todo se encamino a
reducir al Principe de Orange para que diese la mano a la paz, y que lo ha conseguido; pues desde este ultimo correo
de Holanda esta este Rey mas gustoso y satisfecho de dicho Principe, y el embajador Van Beuningen no menos, con su
inclinacion tan descarada a este fin.

Pero aun no es este todo el mal, pues si se encaminaran esos oficios por la senda derecha de la honra, pudieramos
esperar un buen ajuste para todos; pero temo, y no sin grandes fundamentos, que entre este Rey y holandeses nos han
[?proyectado] la paz que nos quieren dar, y que ha sido menester vencer al de Orange y ajustar sus intereses; con cuya
negociacion, y la prisa que se dan estos en Nimega para llegar a la sustancia, pasando sobre tantos inconvenientes y
formalidades de disgusto para las partes, van de carrera a la conclusion, y se prometen aqui conseguirla antes de la
canpaña, por lo que le importa a este Rey y por lo que lo desean holandeses. En cuyo proyecto temo siempre que nos
quitan la Vorgoña, porque, haviendose pasado grandes discursos conmigo sobre esta materia, en que me vieron firme y
asentar por primer fundamento de la paz la restitucion de esta provincia, se han recatado de mi; y por ultimo andan
grandes demandas y respuestas de aqui a Paris.

Sobre que hable dos dias ha al Rey, y me dijo que la paz se habia de hacer, que el ni estava en estado de entrar en
guerra ni la queria, que Flandes habia de quedar con fronteras, que no nos lisonjeasemos con el Parlamento, porque
este no le obligaria a este empeño. En que dire a V.S. que las disposiciones que tenemos para este tribunal no pueden
ser mejores, pero por la misma razon temo que ha de haber tanta confusion que ha de obligar a este Rey a disolverle o
prorrogarle, con que nos quedaremos como nos estamos. No obstante, no pierdo de vista todos los oficios que puedan
fomentar las esperanzas; y para el punto principal, y que nos mira tan de cerca, el de las levas y reclutas para
Francia, he proseguido mis quejas sin rendirme al temor que me quisieron poner con la respuesta disparatada que se me
dio, en que no obstante se logro a lo menos el que en el reino de Inglaterra cesasen las reclutas; pero prosiguiendo en
Escocia, escribi el papel de que va copia adjunta al secretario de Estado, cuya respuesta vera V.S., tambien con la
carta que Su Magestad Britanica mando escribir a los estados de Escocia.

Vi lo que V.S. me participa del discurso que le tuvo Monsieur de Amerongen, y es lastima que una respuesta tan discreta
y prudente como la de V.S. se enplease en aquel sujeto, porque no es de los mas clasicos de Holanda, aunque es de los de
la mala intencion. Su dictamen no confronta con el de Van Beuningen, que esta aqui, pues este no es de la opinion de la
tregua, porque es mas [?menagero], y le parece que el mantener los aliados les pudiera causar algun gasto, con que este
se encamina con toda precipitacion a la paz; y, como V.S. dice, cualquiera sera mejor que una tregua. Pero lo que yo
creo es que, si faltase aqui a la abertura de la canpaña tiempo para concluir la paz, pudiera este Rey, instado de este
ministro, proponer alguna suspension de armas de dos o tres meses, pero mas no. Lo que puedo asegurar a V.S. es que
antes de un mes ha de reventar la postema, y que de todo lo que fuere ocurriendo le dare cuenta mas puntualmente,
solicitando siempre [de] [V.S.] las ordenes de su servicio.

## Notes

- R972 is a duplicate DECODE record of R971: its seven images (5034-5040) are pixel-identical to R971's (5027-5033), and
  both records date the letter Londres 26 January 1677. The reading above is the same as read/R971_reading.md; it was
  checked again against R972's own cipher transcription, which is cleaner than R971's (key decode 95.0% / sense 89.3%
  against 95.4% / 82.1%) and agrees with the margin word for word where both are legible.
- Code values that R972's transcription shows clearly, confirmed by the margin: `dos` = Münster, `fan` = oficio(s)
  ("esos oficios"; also R966), `cos` = honra, `bes` = espera (also R967, R969), `90` = ajuste (2: ajuste, ajustar),
  `fis` = negociación (cf. negocio R966/R967), `cil` (+ r+ se s) = holandeses (4), `116` = cartas, `buf` = gobierna,
  `C_` = trabajo, `140` = particular(mente) (also R966), `dis` = monsieur.
- `163`: both DECODE transcriptions read 163 in "por la misma [163] temo"; the margin has "razón". keyx.py has 163 =
  suecia; either the sign is misread or 163 is razón here. One place; reported, not entered.
- "nos han [?proyectado] la paz": figures `ag y 26 68 51 70 48` = ha-n-p-ro-[51]-ta-do; 51 is a null in keyx.py. The
  margin word is not clear on the scan ("projectado"/"profesado"). Later "en cuyo proyecto" is spelled in the figures
  pro-to-mo..., so the decipherer's word is uncertain too.
- "[?menagero]": figures me-na-[r?]-o, margin "menageo". "[?nuestra] lira": figures an-da-nue-li-ra.

<!-- English translation of read/R972_reading.md (same letter as R971) (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R972, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 26 Jan 1677

⟨... and that all my obedience to your service wishes to deserve it.⟩ I do not wonder how awkward that residence must
be for Your Lordship, having to deal with such different humours and interests, and particularly with the Bishop of
Münster, whom I take for a quibbler and, as they say, something of a cheat; but let Your Lordship console himself that
there is trouble everywhere, and let him turn round to the north and he will see how [?our] lyre plays, what is
happening at Nijmegen and what is happening here, the disorder proceeding from the pilot who steers the helm, for
which, the voices of the people say, there is another who alone can remedy it; on which the letters from Madrid will
undeceive Your Lordship.

Don Pedro Ronquillo has been anxious these days, with motives to spare; and although my reports will not have freed
him of it, he will nevertheless proceed with more light, for since his fears came from the journey of Ambassador
Temple to The Hague, which gave him and everyone cause for suspicion, I have tried to discover all the ground I could,
and I have found that everything was aimed at bringing the Prince of Orange round to lend a hand to the peace, and that
it has succeeded; for since this last post from Holland this King is better pleased and satisfied with the said Prince,
and Ambassador Van Beuningen no less, with his inclination so barefaced to this end.

But even this is not the whole evil, for if those offices were directed along the straight path of honour, we could
hope for a good settlement for all; but I fear, and not without great grounds, that between this King and the Dutch
they have [?planned] for us the peace they mean to give us, and that it has been necessary to win over the [Prince] of
Orange and settle his interests; with which negotiation, and the haste these men are making at Nijmegen to come to the
substance, passing over so many difficulties and formalities displeasing to the parties, they are running to the
conclusion, and they promise themselves here to obtain it before the campaign, because of what it matters to this King
and because the Dutch desire it. In which project I always fear they take Burgundy from us, because, great discussions
having passed with me on this matter, in which they saw me firm and laying down as the first foundation of the peace
the restitution of this province, they have kept themselves guarded from me; and lately there are great demands and
answers going from here to Paris.

On which I spoke two days ago to the King, and he told me that the peace had to be made, that he was neither in a
state to enter a war nor wanted it, that Flanders had to remain with frontiers, that we should not flatter ourselves
with the Parliament, because it would not oblige him to this engagement. On which I will tell Your Lordship that the
dispositions we have towards this tribunal could not be better, but for the same reason I fear there must be so much
confusion that it will oblige this King to dissolve or prorogue it, so that we shall remain as we are. Nevertheless I
do not lose sight of any offices that may foster our hopes; and on the main point, which touches us so closely, that
of the levies and recruits for France, I have pursued my complaints without yielding to the fear they tried to put in
me with the absurd answer I was given, in which nevertheless it was achieved at least that the recruiting ceased in the
kingdom of England; but as it continued in Scotland, I wrote the paper of which a copy goes enclosed to the Secretary
of State, whose answer Your Lordship will see, together with the letter His Britannic Majesty ordered written to the
Estates of Scotland.

I saw what Your Lordship tells me of the discourse Monsieur d'Amerongen held with you, and it is a pity that so discreet
and prudent an answer as Your Lordship's should be spent on that person, for he is not one of the most classic men of
Holland, though he is one of those of ill intention. His opinion does not agree with that of Van Beuningen, who is
here, for the latter is not of the opinion for the truce, because he is more [?thrifty], and he thinks that maintaining
the allies might cost them some expense, so that he is heading with all haste towards the peace; and, as Your Lordship
says, any [peace] will be better than a truce. But what I believe is that, if time were lacking here at the opening of
the campaign to conclude the peace, this King, urged by this minister, might propose some suspension of arms for two
or three months, but no more. What I can assure Your Lordship is that within a month the abscess must burst, and that
of everything that occurs I will give you a more punctual account, always seeking [Your Lordship's] orders for your
service.

R973, Salinas, 2 Mar 1677

Salinas to Fuenmayor, London 2 Mar 1677: Parliament's first steps on recalling the English troops from France look promising; with money more could be done, since "todo va de cabeza y de limosna"; talk of the Spanish negotiations has calmed; if Louis XIV left on Sunday the march from Paris is made.

# R973, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 2 Mar 1677

⟨... de cuyas novedades remito a V.S. relacion, que continuare de lo que pasare todos los dias,⟩ no siendo tan malos
principios quando los dan en negocio que tanto nos importa como retirar las tropas de Francia, en que me prometo
persistiran; negociacion que no es despreciable quando se consigue a tan poca costa como la que yo les hago, pero con
alguna se pudieran lograr cosas mayores, en que no ha vastado la prevencion de muchos meses, con que todo va de caveza
y de limosna. Los discursos de nuestras negociaciones han calmado por aora.

⟨... pero me deja cuidadoso la marcha del Rey de Francia, que aseguran salio domingo,⟩ que, siendo cierto, la de
Paris lleva hecha.

## Notes

- Margin found: 5041 carries a full contemporary decipherment in the left margin (the last line runs across the foot
  of the page), and 5042 continues it and glosses the last cipher passage. DECODE described it in a comment and took
  the last gloss for clear text, so the letter had been listed as one without a decipherment.
- Margin and key decode agree word for word; every word is carried by both, except "de París" and the first syllable
  of "lleva", which only the margin gives (the cipher has one code sign there).
- The B-like sign DECODE could not identify is the key's s, eight times. Label fixes and code words are in
  R973_corrections.tsv and R973_reread_log.md.
- "siendo cierto": the cipher has "cierto", the margin "cierta" (agreeing with "la marcha").
- "meses": the cipher spells it with a struck syllable; the margin reads "meses".
- The two short marks "nad cit" before the first cipher group are not deciphered and are left out.
- "Las tropas de Francia": the English troops serving in the French army, whose recall the Parliament then sitting
  pressed on Charles II.
Translation

R973_en.md

<!-- English translation of read/R973_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R973, Salinas to Fuenmayor, London, 2 Mar 1677

⟨... of whose news I send Your Lordship an account, which I shall continue with what happens every day,⟩ these being
not such bad beginnings, since they make them in a business that matters so much to us as withdrawing the troops from
France, in which I expect they will persist; a negotiation not to be despised when it is achieved at as little cost as
the one I am making them, though with some [money] greater things could be achieved, in which the provision of many
months has not been enough, so that everything goes headlong and on charity. The talk about our negotiations has
calmed down for now.

⟨... but the King of France's march worries me; they say he left on Sunday,⟩ for, if that is true, he has already made
the march from Paris.

R974, Ronquillo, 21 Sep 1676

Ronquillo to Fuenmayor, The Hague 21 Sep 1676: Stralsund and Philippsburg; the Münster and Celle troops better sent to Pomerania; mocks the Council of State; France's declaration of war on Denmark; the Bremen assembly; remittances must be in Antwerp by 10 November or "Maese Luis" will cook the peace.

# R974, Ronquillo to Fuenmayor, The Hague, 21 Sept 1676

## Reading

⟨No se confirma la toma de Wabur ... dificulto que esta tome.⟩ Igual fuera que Stralsund cayera, porque acabariamos
con suecos en Alemania, y pleiteariamos sobre cosa hecha, como el señor Emperador lo hara desde Philisbourg. Y viendo
lo adelantado del tiempo y poca disposicion para que las tropas de Munster y Zell obren en la Mosela, fuera arto mejor
que se fueran todas a Pomerania; pero es menester seguir en esto como en lo demas el destino.

⟨Que una suegra con una hija haga cien yernos, en todas partes lo vemos, ...⟩ pero que en España quieran, sin pagar
subsidios, que es lo mismo que sin [?himeneo] tener parte en las conquistas del norte, es muy propio de la grande
habilidad de nuestro Consejo de Estado. Lo cierto es que pende del empeño que tomare el Rey de Francia por suecos,
porque de otra suerte no sabra con quien hacen el trueque de la parte imaginada que puede tocarnos. Y me parece que sin
duda el Rey de Francia ha gastado medio pliego de papel en la declaracion de la guerra contra Dinamarca para decir a
los suecos que ha hecho cuanto ha podido, pero que ellos no se an ayudado; con esta amenaza arma negociacion con
Dinamarca y Brandeburgo. V.S. ha obrado con grande atencion con el ministro del Duque de Zell; y aunque el acuerdo que
ha hecho este principe con el Obispo de Munster le llaman provisional, yo le tengo entre ellos por muy decisivo, y en
todo caso siempre sera vien que en la asanblea de Bremen se remita esto al congreso, y que V.S. tome la pena de
tenerme instruido de las maximas de esos potentados; porque en España dicen que se saque la parte por lo que ha
inportado la real autoridad de Su Majestad, y haberlo armado a su costa, para las conquistas que han hecho.

⟨El Principe de Orange se fue muy aprisa a Brabante ... De todo esto informara a V.S. don Manuel de Lira, y yo me
contentare con decirle⟩ que estos caprichos de hombre mozo le an de costar caro; y asi esto como toda la reparacion de
la monarquia consiste en que para 10 de novienbre esten en Anveres prontas y efectivas remesas para paladear los
aliados, y para que el gobernador de Flandes tenga un ejercito de 20 mil hombres desenvarazado de la gente de las
plazas y prevenido de todo lo necesario para poder campear; porque de otra forma no continuaran los aliados en los
gastos de la guerra, y haremos una paz como nos la quisiere guisar Maese Luis.

## Notes

- Date: the clear text dates the letter "Haya y septiembre 21 de 1676", not 21 December (DECODE's catalogue name says
  1676Dec21). The content fits September: Philippsburg had just fallen to the Imperials (17 Sept 1676), and remittances
  are wanted "para 10 de noviembre". Ronquillo writes from The Hague: he says he is held there and cannot yet appear
  decently at Nijmegen.
- The margin decipherment runs beside every cipher line on ff. 1-3 and continues under the last cipher line of f. 1
  between the clear lines ("mejor que se fueran todas a Pomerania, pero es menester seguir en esto como en lo demás el
  destino"). It was read from the images in full; DECODE's margin transcription is garbled ("strarfund", "abinpo de
  Murester", "mire luis") and partly drops lines.
- The key decode agrees with the margin wherever it can be read: "acavar amos con su cosen ya y pley te ara mos" =
  acabaríamos con suecos en [Alemania] y pleitearíamos; "lo hara desde filis burg"; "que se fu ran todas a pome[ra]nia
  pero es menester seguir en esto como en lo demas el destino"; "la asanblea de bremen"; "tenerme ... de las ma[xi]mas
  de esos potentados"; "a su costa para las conquistas que han hecho"; "para paladear los aliados".
- Code words confirmed by the margin: `dos` = Münster (also R966, R971); `bo` = empeño (margin "pende del empeño"; also
  R971); `or` = Dinamarca; `in`/`i b` = España (2 here); `11` = consejo (de Estado) here; `bil` = forma ("de otra forma",
  1 here).
- "sin [?himeneo]": the margin writes "sin hime)" (an abbreviation), the figures give si-a-me; read with the joke of the
  preceding clear sentence (a mother-in-law with one daughter making a hundred sons-in-law), i.e. a share in the
  conquests without the marriage. Uncertain.
- "Maese Luis": margin "[..]re Luis", key decode "maestre"; Louis XIV as the cook who will dish up the peace.
- "hombre mozo" is the Prince of Orange (26), whose dash to Brabant ending at Soestdijk is told in clear just before.
Translation

R974_en.md

<!-- English translation of read/R974_reading.md (Claude Opus 5.5, 22 Oct 2026). Words in ⟨ ⟩ are in clear in the letter. -->

# R974, Ronquillo to Fuenmayor, The Hague, 21 Sept 1676

⟨The taking of Wabur is not confirmed ... I doubt this one will take it.⟩ It would be all the same if Stralsund fell,
for we would be finished with the Swedes in Germany, and we would be litigating over a thing already done, as the Lord
Emperor will do from Philippsburg. And seeing how far the season is advanced and how little disposition there is for
the troops of Münster and Celle to act on the Moselle, it would be much better for them all to go to Pomerania; but in
this, as in everything else, one must follow destiny.

⟨That a mother-in-law with one daughter should make a hundred sons-in-law, we see everywhere, ...⟩ but that in Spain
they should want, without paying subsidies, which is the same as having a share in the conquests of the North without
[?the marriage], is very much in keeping with the great skill of our Council of State. What is certain is that it
depends on the commitment the King of France makes for the Swedes, for otherwise he will not know with whom they are
trading the imaginary share that may fall to us. And it seems to me that the King of France has no doubt spent half a
sheet of paper on the declaration of war against Denmark in order to tell the Swedes that he has done all he could,
but that they have not helped themselves; with this threat he arms a negotiation with Denmark and Brandenburg. Your
Lordship has acted with great consideration towards the minister of the Duke of Celle; and although the agreement this
prince has made with the Bishop of Münster is called provisional, I hold it among them to be very decisive, and in any
case it will always be well that at the assembly of Bremen this be referred to the congress, and that Your Lordship
take the trouble to keep me informed of the maxims of those potentates; for in Spain they say that the share should be
drawn according to what the royal authority of His Majesty has counted for, and his having armed it at his own expense,
for the conquests they have made.

⟨The Prince of Orange went off in great haste to Brabant ... Don Manuel de Lira will inform Your Lordship of all this,
and I shall content myself with telling you⟩ that these whims of a young man will cost him dear; and both this and the
whole recovery of the monarchy depend on there being in Antwerp, by 10 November, ready and effective remittances to
sweeten the allies, and on the governor of Flanders having an army of 20,000 men freed from the garrisons of the
fortresses and provided with everything needed to take the field; for otherwise the allies will not continue in the
costs of the war, and we shall make a peace such as Master Louis pleases to cook for us.

R975, Ronquillo, 28 Dec 1676

Ronquillo to Fuenmayor, Nijmegen 28 Dec 1676: the Dutch press for peace; Kinsky's sciatica excuse; full powers handed to the mediators; objections to the Swedish and French powers (Westphalia clause, Fürstenberg's release, the Pope's mediation); Münster and Wolfenbüttel over Bremen; France denies Lorraine and the princes the jus legationis.

Transcription: DOC_R975_D2848_2848.txt
